I know that’s not ideal.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these mistakes and forgetful moments are significantly interfering with your daily life or overall well-being?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, probably more than I’d like to admit. I try to tell myself it’s not a huge deal, but I have turned things in late, and I’ve missed a couple application deadlines because I just didn’t stay on top of them. One of my mentors also told me my follow-through is starting to hurt opportunities I should be ready for, which was… kind of hard to hear. So I guess it is interfering, at least in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a big way. I mean, I might shift around or tap my foot a little if I’ve been sitting for a long time, but I’ve never really thought of myself as restless. Is that what you mean, like not being able to stay seated?\n\nDoctor: Do you notice that your mistakes or forgetfulness happen more often when you're under stress or experiencing strong emotions?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure, honestly. Does that answer what you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.8, F90.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Um… I guess what finally pushed me to come in is that I’ve been making these small mistakes in things I turn in—applications, research notes, stuff like that. Nothing huge, I don’t think, but embarrassing… like getting an artwork date wrong or missing a question even though I really thought I’d checked it. It’s been happening enough that I figured I should ask someone about it, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're putting in the same amount of effort and attention to detail as you used to when working on these tasks?\n\nPatient: I think I’m trying to, yeah. Like, I don’t feel like I’m just being careless on purpose or rushing through everything. I’ll honestly think I checked something over, and then later I find out I skipped a question on an application or put the wrong date for an artwork in my notes. It’s small stuff, mostly, but it’s embarrassing because I really did think I was paying attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget things that you would normally remember?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not with big important things usually, but normal day-to-day stuff, I guess. Like I’ll mean to return an email, or pay a bill, or pick up groceries, and then a few days later I realize I just… never did it. It kind of drops out of my mind. I don’t know if that counts as “often,” but it happens enough that it’s frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious, sad, or overwhelmed by these mistakes and forgetfulness?\n\nPatient: Um… I wouldn’t say sad, exactly. Maybe a little anxious or embarrassed when I notice it, especially if someone else points it out. But I don’t want to overstate it—it’s not like I’m falling apart or anything. It’s more just frustrating, and then I start worrying I’m going to miss something again. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus or concentrate on tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sometimes. I mean, I can focus if it’s quiet and I’m already into it, but when I’m trying to read or write at my desk, it doesn’t take much to pull me off track. Like a siren outside, or someone talking in the hallway, or even just some random thought about an exhibit idea, and then I’ll realize twenty minutes went by and I haven’t gotten back to what I was doing. I guess that probably counts, right?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your mood changes significantly in response to these small mistakes or forgetful moments?\n\nPatient: Um… not significantly, I don’t think. I might feel a little annoyed with myself or embarrassed for a bit, especially if someone notices, but it usually passes. I wouldn’t say my mood swings a lot because of it. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain tasks or situations because you're worried about making mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess a little. I don’t think I avoid them because I’m terrified of mistakes, exactly, but if I know something is going to take a lot of concentration—like drafting a curatorial proposal or getting through a dense theory chapter—I’ll kind of… push it off. I’ll do smaller errands first, like email or tidying things up, and then suddenly it’s the night before it’s due. I know that’s not ideal.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these mistakes and forgetful moments are significantly interfering with your daily life or overall well-being?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, probably more than I’d like to admit. I try to tell myself it’s not a huge deal, but I have turned things in late, and I’ve missed a couple application deadlines because I just didn’t stay on top of them. One of my mentors also told me my follow-through is starting to hurt opportunities I should be ready for, which was… kind of hard to hear. So I guess it is interfering, at least in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a big way. I mean, I might shift around or tap my foot a little if I’ve been sitting for a long time, but I’ve never really thought of myself as restless. Is that what you mean, like not being able to stay seated?\n\nDoctor: Do you notice that your mistakes or forgetfulness happen more often when you're under stress or experiencing strong emotions?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure, honestly. It’s been maybe almost two years now — about a year and eleven months, if I’m remembering right. I don’t know if that’s the kind of timeframe you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they've finished speaking?\n\nPatient: Not really, I don’t think that’s a big issue for me. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

Yeah… I think so. I mean, I can get things done eventually, but organizing them is kind of messy for me. My apartment and my laptop both end up looking like a bunch of half-started projects — rent papers, gallery notes, article PDFs, random to-do lists everywhere.

It’s been happening enough that I figured I should ask someone about it, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're putting in the same amount of effort and attention to detail as you used to when working on these tasks?\n\nPatient: I think I’m trying to, yeah. Like, I don’t feel like I’m just being careless on purpose or rushing through everything. I’ll honestly think I checked something over, and then later I find out I skipped a question on an application or put the wrong date for an artwork in my notes. It’s small stuff, mostly, but it’s embarrassing because I really did think I was paying attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget things that you would normally remember?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not with big important things usually, but normal day-to-day stuff, I guess. Like I’ll mean to return an email, or pay a bill, or pick up groceries, and then a few days later I realize I just… never did it. It kind of drops out of my mind. I don’t know if that counts as “often,” but it happens enough that it’s frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious, sad, or overwhelmed by these mistakes and forgetfulness?\n\nPatient: Um… I wouldn’t say sad, exactly. Maybe a little anxious or embarrassed when I notice it, especially if someone else points it out. But I don’t want to overstate it—it’s not like I’m falling apart or anything. It’s more just frustrating, and then I start worrying I’m going to miss something again. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus or concentrate on tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sometimes. I mean, I can focus if it’s quiet and I’m already into it, but when I’m trying to read or write at my desk, it doesn’t take much to pull me off track. I guess that probably counts, right?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your mood changes significantly in response to these small mistakes or forgetful moments?\n\nPatient: Um… not significantly, I don’t think. I might feel a little annoyed with myself or embarrassed for a bit, especially if someone notices, but it usually passes. I wouldn’t say my mood swings a lot because of it. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain tasks or situations because you're worried about making mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess a little. I don’t think I avoid them because I’m terrified of mistakes, exactly, but if I know something is going to take a lot of concentration—like drafting a curatorial proposal or getting through a dense theory chapter—I’ll kind of… push it off. I’ll do smaller errands first, like email or tidying things up, and then suddenly it’s the night before it’s due. Nothing huge, I don’t think, but embarrassing… like getting an artwork date wrong or missing a question even though I really thought I’d checked it. It’s been happening enough that I figured I should ask someone about it, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're putting in the same amount of effort and attention to detail as you used to when working on these tasks?\n\nPatient: I think I’m trying to, yeah. Like, I don’t feel like I’m just being careless on purpose or rushing through everything. I’ll honestly think I checked something over, and then later I find out I skipped a question on an application or put the wrong date for an artwork in my notes. It’s small stuff, mostly, but it’s embarrassing because I really did think I was paying attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget things that you would normally remember?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not with big important things usually, but normal day-to-day stuff, I guess. Like I’ll mean to return an email, or pay a bill, or pick up groceries, and then a few days later I realize I just… never did it. It kind of drops out of my mind. I don’t know if that counts as “often,” but it happens enough that it’s frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious, sad, or overwhelmed by these mistakes and forgetfulness?\n\nPatient: Um… I wouldn’t say sad, exactly. Maybe a little anxious or embarrassed when I notice it, especially if someone else points it out. But I don’t want to overstate it—it’s not like I’m falling apart or anything. It’s more just frustrating, and then I start worrying I’m going to miss something again. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus or concentrate on tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sometimes. I mean, I can focus if it’s quiet and I’m already into it, but when I’m trying to read or write at my desk, it doesn’t take much to pull me off track. Like a siren outside, or someone talking in the hallway, or even just some random thought about an exhibit idea, and then I’ll realize twenty minutes went by and I haven’t gotten back to what I was doing. I guess that probably counts, right?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your mood changes significantly in response to these small mistakes or forgetful moments?\n\nPatient: Um… not significantly, I don’t think. I might feel a little annoyed with myself or embarrassed for a bit, especially if someone notices, but it usually passes. I wouldn’t say my mood swings a lot because of it. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain tasks or situations because you're worried about making mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess a little. I don’t think I avoid them because I’m terrified of mistakes, exactly, but if I know something is going to take a lot of concentration—like drafting a curatorial proposal or getting through a dense theory chapter—I’ll kind of… push it off. I’ll do smaller errands first, like email or tidying things up, and then suddenly it’s the night before it’s due. I know that’s not ideal.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these mistakes and forgetful moments are significantly interfering with your daily life or overall well-being?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, probably more than I’d like to admit. I try to tell myself it’s not a huge deal, but I have turned things in late, and I’ve missed a couple application deadlines because I just didn’t stay on top of them. One of my mentors also told me my follow-through is starting to hurt opportunities I should be ready for, which was… kind of hard to hear. So I guess it is interfering, at least in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a big way. I mean, I might shift around or tap my foot a little if I’ve been sitting for a long time, but I’ve never really thought of myself as restless. Is that what you mean, like not being able to stay seated?\n\nDoctor: Do you notice that your mistakes or forgetfulness happen more often when you're under stress or experiencing strong emotions?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure, honestly. I haven’t really tracked it that closely. I guess when I’m stressed I probably get a little more scattered, but I don’t know if it’s a clear pattern or just… normal stress making things harder. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing these mistakes and forgetful moments for a short period, or has it been going on for a while?\n\nPatient: It’s been going on for a while, I think. I first really noticed it after I moved to Boston, after finishing my bachelor’s, so… not just the past few weeks or anything. It’s been maybe almost two years now — about a year and eleven months, if I’m remembering right. I don’t know if that’s the kind of timeframe you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they've finished speaking?\n\nPatient: Not really, I don’t think that’s a big issue for me. I mean, I might accidentally talk over someone once in a while, like anyone does, but I don’t usually blurt things out or cut people off a lot. At least, I don’t think people would describe me that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any periods where these mistakes and forgetful moments stopped happening or significantly decreased?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, it’s not like it’s been terrible every single day, but I can’t think of a time where it fully stopped or clearly got much better. It’s been kind of noticeable since I moved to Boston after finishing my bachelor’s, so… about a year and eleven months now, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty organizing tasks and activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I can get things done eventually, but organizing them is kind of messy for me. My apartment and my laptop both end up looking like a bunch of half-started projects — rent papers, gallery notes, article PDFs, random to-do lists everywhere.\n\nAnd then I sort of… don’t know what actually needs to happen first. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it does get in the way sometimes. Does that count as difficulty organizing?\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties with organization, focus, and forgetfulness been present for most of the past two years, or have they come and gone?\n\nPatient: Mostly present, I think. It’s not like it’s been equally bad every week or anything, but I can’t really point to a period where it went away. It’s been noticeable since I moved to Boston after finishing my bachelor’s, so… about a year and eleven months now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

I don’t think I avoid them because I’m terrified of mistakes, exactly, but if I know something is going to take a lot of concentration—like drafting a curatorial proposal or getting through a dense theory chapter—I’ll kind of… push it off. I’ll do smaller errands first, like email or tidying things up, and then suddenly it’s the night before it’s due. I know that’s not ideal.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these mistakes and forgetful moments are significantly interfering with your daily life or overall well-being?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, probably more than I’d like to admit. I try to tell myself it’s not a huge deal, but I have turned things in late, and I’ve missed a couple application deadlines because I just didn’t stay on top of them. One of my mentors also told me my follow-through is starting to hurt opportunities I should be ready for, which was… kind of hard to hear. So I guess it is interfering, at least in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a big way. I mean, I might shift around or tap my foot a little if I’ve been sitting for a long time, but I’ve never really thought of myself as restless. Is that what you mean, like not being able to stay seated?\n\nDoctor: Do you notice that your mistakes or forgetfulness happen more often when you're under stress or experiencing strong emotions?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure, honestly.
